# Tennessee

This article is about the U.S. state. For the river, see [Tennessee River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_River). For other uses, see [Tennessee (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_\(disambiguation\)).

"Tenn" redirects here. For the Japanese MC, see [Tenn (MC)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenn_\(MC\)).

**Tennessee** ([/ˌtɛnɪˈsiː/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/b/b0/En-us-Tennessee.ogg/En-us-Tennessee.ogg.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/b/b0/En-us-Tennessee.ogg/En-us-Tennessee.ogg.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup>, locally [/ˈtɛnɪsi/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English)),[^cite_note-11][^cite_note-12][^cite_note-EPD-13] officially the **State of Tennessee**, is a landlocked [state](18618239.md) in the [Southeastern](816925.md) region of the [United States](3434750.md). It borders [Kentucky](16846.md) to the north, [Virginia](32432.md) to the northeast, [North Carolina](21650.md) to the east, [Georgia](48830.md), [Alabama](303.md), and [Mississippi](16949861.md) to the south, [Arkansas](1930.md) to the southwest, and [Missouri](19571.md) to the northwest. Tennessee is the [36th-largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_area) by area and the [15th-most populous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_population) of the 50 states. According to the [United States Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Census_Bureau), the state's estimated population as of 2024 is 7.22 million.[^cite_note-census.gov-7]

Tennessee is geographically, culturally, and legally divided into three [Grand Divisions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Divisions_of_Tennessee) of [East](646800.md), [Middle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Tennessee), and [West Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Tennessee). [Nashville](22018.md) is the state's capital and most populous city, and anchors its largest metropolitan area. Tennessee has diverse terrain and landforms, and from east to west, contains a mix of cultural features characteristic of [Appalachia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appalachia), the [Upland South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upland_South), and the [Deep South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deep_South). The [Blue Ridge Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Ridge_Mountains) along the eastern border reach some of the highest elevations in eastern North America, and the [Cumberland Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumberland_Plateau) contains many scenic valleys and [waterfalls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waterfall). The central part of the state is marked by cavernous bedrock and irregular rolling hills, and level, fertile plains define West Tennessee. The state is twice bisected by the [Tennessee River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_River), and the [Mississippi River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_River) forms its western border. The [Great Smoky Mountains National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Smoky_Mountains_National_Park), the nation's most visited national park, is in eastern Tennessee.[^cite_note-14]

Tennessee is rooted in the [Watauga Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watauga_Association), a 1772 frontier pact generally regarded as the first constitutional government west of the [Appalachian Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appalachian_Mountains).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinger200146–47-15] Its name derives from *[Tanasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanasi)* (ᏔᎾᏏ), a [Cherokee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cherokee) town preceding the first European American settlement.[^cite_note-16] Tennessee was initially part of North Carolina, and later the [Southwest Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwest_Territory), before its admission to the Union as the 16th state on June 1, 1796. It earned the nickname "The Volunteer State" due to a strong tradition of military service.[^cite_note-culturetrip-17] A [slave state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_states_and_free_states) until the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War), Tennessee was politically divided, with most of its western and middle parts supporting the [Confederacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederate_States_of_America), and most of the eastern region harboring [pro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Unionist)-[Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_\(American_Civil_War\)) sentiment. As a result, Tennessee was the last state to officially [secede](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secession_in_the_United_States) from the Union and join the Confederacy, and the first former Confederate state readmitted to the Union after the war had ended during the [Reconstruction era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconstruction_era).[^cite_note-guide-18]

During the 20th century, Tennessee transitioned from a predominantly [agrarian society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agrarian_society) to a more diversified economy. This was aided in part by massive federal investment in the [Tennessee Valley Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_Valley_Authority) (TVA) and the city of [Oak Ridge](151055.md), which was established during [World War II](32927.md) to house the [Manhattan Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manhattan_Project)'s uranium enrichment facilities for the construction of the [world's first atomic bombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_nuclear_weapons). After the war, the [Oak Ridge National Laboratory](38147.md) became a key center of scientific research. The state's economy is dominated by the [health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_industry), [music](171080.md#Industry_and_economics), [finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_services), [automotive](23576520.md), [chemical](58721.md), [electronics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronics_industry_in_the_United_States), and [tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism) sectors, and [cattle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cattle), [soybeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soybean), [poultry](23197.md), [corn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maize), and [cotton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cotton) are its primary agricultural products.[^cite_note-homeandfarm13-19] Tennessee has played a major role in the development of many forms of [popular music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_music), including [country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Country_music), [blues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blues_music), [rock and roll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_and_roll), [soul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soul_music), and [gospel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_music).

## Etymology

Main article: [Name of Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Name_of_Tennessee)

Drawing of Tanasi, Tennessee's namesake, by Henry Timberlake

Detail of [Tanasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanasi) (spelled "Tennessee") on [Henry Timberlake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Timberlake)'s *Draught of the Cherokee Country*

Tennessee derives its name most directly from the [Cherokee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cherokee) town of [Tanasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanasi) (or "Tanase", in [syllabary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cherokee_syllabary): ᏔᎾᏏ) in present-day [Monroe County, Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monroe_County,_Tennessee), on the Tanasi River, now known as the [Little Tennessee River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Tennessee_River). In 1567, [Spanish explorer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conquistador) Captain [Juan Pardo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Pardo_\(explorer\)) and his party encountered a [Native American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States) village named "Tanasqui" in the area while traveling inland from modern-day [South Carolina](27956.md); however, it is unknown if this was the same settlement as Tanasi.[^cite_note-21] The town appeared on British maps as early as 1725. Recent research suggests that the Cherokees adapted the name from the [Yuchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuchi) word *Tana-tsee-dgee*, meaning "brother-waters-place" or "where-the-waters-meet".[^cite_note-McBride_THQ_1971-22][^cite_note-23][^cite_note-Woktela,_Who_Were_the_Yuchi-24] The modern spelling, *Tennessee*, is attributed to Governor [James Glen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Glen) of South Carolina, who used this spelling in his official correspondence during the 1750s. In 1788, North Carolina created "[Tennessee County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_County,_North_Carolina)", and in 1796, a [constitutional convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_convention_\(political_meeting\)), organizing the new state from the [Southwest Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwest_Territory), adopted "Tennessee" as the state's name.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELangsdon200023-25]

## History

Main article: [History of Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Tennessee)

### Pre-European era

The first inhabitants of Tennessee were [Paleo-Indians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleo-Indians) who arrived about 12,000 years ago at the end of the [Last Glacial Period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Glacial_Period). Archaeological excavations indicate that the lower Tennessee Valley was heavily populated by Ice Age [hunter-gatherers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter-gatherer), and Middle Tennessee is believed to have been rich with [game animals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Game_\(hunting\)) such as [mastodons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mastodon).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESatz19793–4-26] The names of the cultural groups who inhabited the area before European contact are unknown, but archaeologists have named several distinct cultural phases, including the [Archaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaic_period_in_the_Americas) (8000–1000 BC), [Woodland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodland_period) (1000 BC–1000 AD), and [Mississippian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippian_culture) (1000–1600 AD) periods.[^cite_note-27] The Archaic peoples first domesticated dogs, and plants such as [squash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cucurbita), [corn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maize), [gourds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gourd), and [sunflowers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunflower) were first grown in Tennessee during the Woodland period.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESatz19794–8-28] Later generations of Woodland peoples constructed the first mounds. Rapid civilizational development occurred during the Mississippian period, when Indigenous peoples developed organized [chiefdoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiefdom) and constructed numerous ceremonial structures throughout the state.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESatz19796–11-29]

Spanish conquistadors who explored the region in the 16th century encountered some of the Mississippian peoples, including the [Muscogee Creek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muscogee), [Yuchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuchi), and [Shawnee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shawnee).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESatz19798–11-30][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell198116–17-31] By the early 18th century, most Natives in Tennessee had disappeared, most likely wiped out by diseases introduced by the Spaniards.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESatz19798–11-30] The Cherokee began migrating into what is now eastern Tennessee from what is now Virginia in the latter 17th century, possibly to escape expanding European settlement and diseases in the north.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESatz197934–35-32] They forced the Creek, Yuchi, and Shawnee out of the state in the early 18th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESatz197934–35-32][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell198118-33] The Chickasaw remained confined to West Tennessee, and the middle part of the state contained few Native Americans, although both the Cherokee and the Shawnee claimed the region as their hunting ground.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESatz197914-34] Cherokee peoples in Tennessee were known by European settlers as the [Overhill Cherokee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overhill_Cherokee) because they lived west of the Blue Ridge Mountains.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinger200126-35] Overhill settlements grew along the rivers in East Tennessee in the early 18th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESatz197944–45-36]

### Exploration and colonization

Further information: [Province of North Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_North_Carolina); [Watauga Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watauga_Association); [Washington District, North Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_District,_North_Carolina); [State of Franklin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_Franklin); and [Southwest Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwest_Territory)

The first recorded European expeditions into what is now Tennessee were led by Spanish explorers [Hernando de Soto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hernando_de_Soto) in 1540–1541, [Tristan de Luna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trist%C3%A1n_de_Luna_y_Arellano) in 1559, and [Juan Pardo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Pardo_\(explorer\)) in 1566–1567.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell198125–26-37][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELangsdon20004–5-38][^cite_note-39] In 1673, English fur trader [Abraham Wood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Wood) sent an expedition from the [Colony of Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony_of_Virginia) into [Overhill Cherokee territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overhill_Cherokee) in modern-day northeastern Tennessee.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinger200120–21-40][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell198127–28-41] That same year, a French expedition led by missionary [Jacques Marquette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Marquette) and [Louis Jolliet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Jolliet) explored the Mississippi River and became the first Europeans to map the Mississippi Valley.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell198127–28-41][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinger200120–21-40] In 1682, an expedition led by [René-Robert Cavelier, Sieur de La Salle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ren%C3%A9-Robert_Cavelier,_Sieur_de_La_Salle) constructed [Fort Prudhomme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Prudhomme) on the [Chickasaw Bluffs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chickasaw_Bluffs) in West Tennessee.[^cite_note-42] By the late 17th century, French traders began to explore the Cumberland River valley, and in 1714, under Charles Charleville's command, established French Lick, a fur trading settlement at the present location of Nashville near the [Cumberland River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumberland_River).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELangsdon20006-43][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlbright190918–19-44] In 1739, the French constructed [Fort Assumption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Assumption) under [Jean-Baptiste Le Moyne de Bienville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Baptiste_Le_Moyne_de_Bienville) on the Mississippi River at the present location of Memphis, which they used as a base against the Chickasaw during the [1739 Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chickasaw_Campaign_of_1739) of the [Chickasaw Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chickasaw_Wars).[^cite_note-45]

refer to caption

Reconstruction of [Fort Loudoun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Loudoun_\(Tennessee\)), the first British settlement in Tennessee

In the 1750s and 1760s, [longhunters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Longhunter) from Virginia explored much of East and Middle Tennessee.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinger200140–42-46] Settlers from the [Colony of South Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony_of_South_Carolina) built [Fort Loudoun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Loudoun_\(Tennessee\)) on the [Little Tennessee River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Tennessee_River) in 1756, the first British settlement in what is now Tennessee and the westernmost British outpost to that date.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinger200135-47][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell198132–33-48] Hostilities erupted between the British and the Cherokees into [an armed conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Cherokee_War), and a [siege of the fort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Fort_Loudoun) ended with its surrender in 1760.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinger200136–37-49] After the [French and Indian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_and_Indian_War), Britain issued the [Royal Proclamation of 1763](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Proclamation_of_1763), which forbade settlements west of the [Appalachian Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appalachian_Mountains) in an effort to mitigate conflicts with the Natives.[^cite_note-50] Migration across the mountains continued, and the first permanent European settlers began arriving in northeastern Tennessee in the late 1760s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELangsdon20008-51][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell198143–44-52] Most of them were [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Americans), but nearly 20% were [Scotch-Irish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotch-Irish_Americans).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell1981106-53] They formed the [Watauga Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watauga_Association) in 1772, a semi-autonomous representative government,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinger200145–47-54] and three years later reorganized themselves into the [Washington District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_District,_North_Carolina) to support the cause of the [American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_theater_of_the_American_Revolutionary_War).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell198160–61-55] The next year, after an unsuccessful petition to Virginia, North Carolina agreed to annex the Washington District to provide protection from Native American attacks.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinger200164–68-56]

In 1775, [Richard Henderson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Henderson_\(jurist\)) negotiated a series of treaties with the Cherokee to sell the lands of the Watauga settlements at [Sycamore Shoals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sycamore_Shoals) on the banks of the [Watauga River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watauga_River). An agreement to sell land for the [Transylvania Colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transylvania_Colony), which included the territory in Tennessee north of the Cumberland River, was also signed.[^cite_note-57] Later that year, [Daniel Boone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Boone), under Henderson's employment, blazed a trail from [Fort Chiswell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Chiswell) in Virginia through the [Cumberland Gap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumberland_Gap), which became part of the [Wilderness Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilderness_Road), a major thoroughfare into Tennessee and Kentucky.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell1981197-58] The Chickamauga, a Cherokee faction loyal to the British led by [Dragging Canoe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dragging_Canoe), opposed the settling of the Washington District and Transylvania Colony, and in 1776 attacked [Fort Watauga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Watauga) at Sycamore Shoals.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell198165–67-59][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESatz197966-60] The warnings of Dragging Canoe's cousin [Nancy Ward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancy_Ward) spared many settlers' lives from the initial attacks.[^cite_note-king07-61] In 1779, [James Robertson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Robertson_\(explorer\)) and [John Donelson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Donelson) led two groups of settlers from the Washington District to the French Lick.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell198153-62] These settlers constructed [Fort Nashborough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Nashborough), which they named for [Francis Nash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Nash), a [brigadier general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brigadier_general_\(United_States\)) of the [Continental Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Army).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlbright190949–50-63] The next year, the settlers signed the [Cumberland Compact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumberland_Compact), which established a representative government for the colony called the [Cumberland Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumberland_Association).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlbright190968–72-64] This settlement later grew into the city of Nashville.[^cite_note-65] That same year [John Sevier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Sevier) led a group of [Overmountain Men](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overmountain_Men) from Fort Watauga to the [Battle of Kings Mountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Kings_Mountain) in South Carolina, where they defeated the British.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinger200184–88-66]

Map of the Southwest Territory in 1790

The Southwest Territory in 1790

Three counties of the [Washington District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_District,_North_Carolina) broke off from [North Carolina](21650.md) in 1784 and formed the [State of Franklin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_Franklin).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell198173–74-67] Efforts to obtain admission to the [Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perpetual_Union) failed, and the counties, now numbering eight, rejoined North Carolina by 1788.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell198181–83-68] North Carolina ceded the area to the federal government in 1790, after which it was organized into the [Southwest Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwest_Territory) on May 26 of that year.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell198186–87-69] The act allowed the territory to petition for statehood once the population reached 60,000.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell198186–87-69] Administration of the territory was divided between the Washington District and the Mero District, the latter of which consisted of the Cumberland Association and was named for Spanish territorial governor [Esteban Rodríguez Miró](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esteban_Rodr%C3%ADguez_Mir%C3%B3).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell198156–57,_90-70] President [George Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington) appointed [William Blount](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Blount) as territorial governor.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELangsdon200016–17-71] The Southwest Territory recorded a population of 35,691 in the [first United States census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1790_United_States_census) that year, including 3,417 slaves.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELamon19804-72]

### Statehood and antebellum era

1796 map of Tennessee by surveyor Daniel Smith

Surveyor [Daniel Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Smith_\(surveyor\))'s "Map of the Tennassee State" (1796)

As support for statehood grew among the settlers, Governor Blount called for elections, which were held in December 1793.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell198193–94-73] The 13-member territorial House of Representatives first convened in Knoxville on February 24, 1794, to select ten members for the legislature's upper house, the council.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell198193–94-73] The full legislature convened on August 25, 1794.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELangsdon200020–21-74] In June 1795, the legislature conducted a census of the territory, which recorded a population of 77,263, including 10,613 slaves, and a poll that showed 6,504 in favor of statehood and 2,562 opposed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell198195-75][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELangsdon200022-76] Elections for a constitutional convention were held in December 1795, and the delegates convened in Knoxville on January 17, 1796, to begin drafting a state constitution.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell198197-77] During this convention, the name Tennessee was chosen for the new state.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELangsdon200023-25] The constitution was completed on February 6, which authorized elections for the state's new legislature, the [Tennessee General Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_General_Assembly).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELangsdon200024-78][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell198199-79] The legislature convened on March 28, 1796, and the next day, John Sevier was announced as the state's first governor.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELangsdon200024-78][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell198199-79] Tennessee was admitted to the Union on June 1, 1796, as the 16th state and the first created from federal territory.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELangsdon200025–26-80][^cite_note-81]

Tennessee reportedly earned the nickname "The Volunteer State" during the [War of 1812](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_1812), when 3,500 Tennesseans answered a recruitment call by the General Assembly for the war effort.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell1981139–140-82] These soldiers, under [Andrew Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Jackson)'s command, played a major role in the American victory at the [Battle of New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_New_Orleans) in 1815, the last major battle of the war.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell1981139–140-82] Several Tennesseans took part in the [Texas Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Revolution) of 1835–36, including Governor [Sam Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Houston) and Congressman and frontiersman [Davy Crockett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Davy_Crockett), who was killed at the [Battle of the Alamo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Alamo).[^cite_note-83] The state's nickname was solidified during the [Mexican–American War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican%E2%80%93American_War) when President [James K. Polk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_K._Polk) issued a call for 2,800 soldiers from the state, and more than 30,000 volunteered.[^cite_note-tohm-84]

President Andrew Jackson's home, The Hermitage, in Nashville

[The Hermitage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hermitage_\(Nashville,_Tennessee\)), plantation home of President [Andrew Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Jackson) in Nashville

Between the 1790s and 1820s, additional land cessions were negotiated with the Cherokee, who had established [a national government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cherokee_Nation_\(1794%E2%80%931907\)) modeled on the [U.S. Constitution](31644.md).[^cite_note-85][^cite_note-86] In 1818, Jackson and Kentucky governor [Isaac Shelby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaac_Shelby) reached an agreement with the Chickasaw to sell the land between the Mississippi and Tennessee Rivers to the United States, which included all of West Tennessee and became known as the "[Jackson Purchase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jackson_Purchase_\(U.S._historical_region\))".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell1981149–150-87] The Cherokee moved their capital from Georgia to the [Red Clay Council Grounds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Clay_State_Park) in southeastern Tennessee in 1832, due to new laws forcing them from their previous capital at [New Echota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Echota).[^cite_note-88] In 1838 and 1839, U.S. troops [forcibly removed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cherokee_removal) thousands of Cherokees and their black slaves from their homes in southeastern Tennessee and forced them to march to [Indian Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Territory) in modern-day [Oklahoma](22489.md). This event is known as the [Trail of Tears](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trail_of_Tears), and an estimated 4,000 died along the way.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESatz1979103-89][^cite_note-90]



in 1946 the U.S. Post Office issued a [commemorative stamp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commemorative_stamp) celebrating the 150th anniversary of Tennessee statehood.

As settlers pushed west of the Cumberland Plateau, a slavery-based [agrarian economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agrarian_economy) took hold in these regions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELamon19809–12-91] Cotton planters used extensive slave labor on large [plantation complexes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plantation_complexes_in_the_Southern_United_States) in West Tennessee's fertile and flat terrain after the Jackson Purchase.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell1981209–212-92] Cotton also took hold in the Nashville Basin during this time.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell1981209–212-92] Entrepreneurs such as [Montgomery Bell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montgomery_Bell) used slaves in the production of iron in the Western Highland Rim, and slaves also cultivated such crops as tobacco and corn throughout the Highland Rim.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELamon19809–12-91] East Tennessee's geography did not allow for large plantations as in the middle and western parts of the state, and as a result, slavery became increasingly rare in the region.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell1981210-93] A strong [abolition movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolitionism_in_the_United_States) developed in East Tennessee, beginning as early as 1797, and in 1819, [Elihu Embree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elihu_Embree) of [Jonesborough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonesborough,_Tennessee) began publishing the *[Manumission Intelligencier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manumission_Intelligencier)* (later *The Emancipator*), the nation's first exclusively anti-slavery newspaper.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELamon19807–9-94][^cite_note-95]

### Civil War

Main articles: [Tennessee in the American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_in_the_American_Civil_War), [Confederate States of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederate_States_of_America), and [Ordinance of Secession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordinance_of_Secession)

At the onset of the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War), most Middle and West Tennesseans favored efforts to preserve their slavery-based economies, but many Middle Tennesseans were initially skeptical of secession. In East Tennessee, most people favored remaining in the Union.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConnelly19793–8-96] In 1860, slaves composed about 25% of Tennessee's population, the lowest share among the states that joined the [Confederacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederate_States_of_America).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELamon1980116-97] Tennessee provided more Union troops than any other Confederate state, and the second-highest number of Confederate troops, behind Virginia.[^cite_note-guide-18][^cite_note-98] Due to its central location, Tennessee was a crucial state during the war and saw more military engagements than any state except Virginia.[^cite_note-99]

After [Abraham Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln) was elected president in [1860](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1860_United_States_presidential_election), secessionists in the state government led by Governor [Isham Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isham_Harris) sought [voter approval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordinance_of_Secession) to sever ties with the United States, which was rejected in a referendum by a 54–46% margin in February 1861.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConnelly19793–4,_291-100] After the Confederate [attack on Fort Sumter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Fort_Sumter) in April and Lincoln's call for troops in response, the legislature ratified an agreement to enter a military league with the Confederacy on May 7, 1861.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConnelly19793–4-101] On June 8, with Middle Tennesseans having significantly changed their position, voters approved a second referendum on secession by a 69–31% margin, becoming the last state to secede.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell1981294-102] In response, East Tennessee Unionists organized [a convention in Knoxville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Tennessee_Convention) with the goal of splitting the region to form a new state loyal to the Union, but this ultimately failed.[^cite_note-103] In the fall of 1861, Unionist guerrillas in East Tennessee [burned bridges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Tennessee_bridge_burnings) and attacked Confederate sympathizers, leading to neighbor against neighbor conflict and the Confederacy to invoke [martial law](50477.md) in parts of the region. Because of this, many southern unionists were sent fleeing to nearby Union states, particularly the [border state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_states_\(American_Civil_War\)) of [Kentucky](16846.md). Other southern unionists, who stayed in Tennessee after the state's secession, either resisted the Confederate cause or eventually joined it.[^cite_note-104] In March 1862, Lincoln appointed [War Democrat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_Democrat) and Tennessean [Andrew Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Johnson) as military governor of the state.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELangsdon2000131-105]

Chromolithograph of the Battle of Franklin, which occurred on November 30, 1864

The [Battle of Franklin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Franklin_\(1864\)), November 30, 1864

General [Ulysses S. Grant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulysses_S._Grant) and the [U.S. Navy](20518076.md) captured the Tennessee and Cumberland rivers in February 1862 at the battles of [Fort Henry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Fort_Henry) and [Fort Donelson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Fort_Donelson).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConnelly197924–30-106] Grant then proceeded south to [Pittsburg Landing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pittsburg_Landing,_Tennessee) and held off a Confederate counterattack at [Shiloh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Shiloh) in April in what was at the time the bloodiest battle of the war.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConnelly197945–51-107] Memphis fell to the Union in June after a [naval battle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Battle_of_Memphis) on the Mississippi River.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConnelly197951–53-108] Union strength in Middle Tennessee was tested in a series of Confederate offensives beginning in the summer of 1862, which culminated in General [William Rosecrans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Rosecrans)'s [Army of the Cumberland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Army_of_the_Cumberland) routing General [Braxton Bragg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Braxton_Bragg)'s [Army of Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Army_of_Tennessee) at [Stones River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Stones_River), another one of the war's costliest engagements.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConnelly197954–65-109] The next summer, Rosecrans's [Tullahoma campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tullahoma_campaign) forced Bragg's remaining troops in Middle Tennessee to retreat to Chattanooga with little fighting.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConnelly197965–68-110]

During the [Chattanooga campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chattanooga_campaign), Confederates attempted to besiege the Army of the Cumberland into surrendering, but reinforcements from the [Army of the Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Army_of_the_Tennessee) under the command of Grant, [William Tecumseh Sherman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Tecumseh_Sherman), and [Joseph Hooker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Hooker) arrived.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConnelly197977–79-111] The Confederates were driven from the city at the battles of [Lookout Mountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Lookout_Mountain) and [Missionary Ridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Missionary_Ridge) in November 1863.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConnelly197980–82-112] Despite Unionist sentiment in East Tennessee, Confederates held the area for most of the war. A few days after the fall of Chattanooga, Confederates led by [James Longstreet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Longstreet) unsuccessfully [campaigned to take control of Knoxville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knoxville_campaign) by attacking Union General [Ambrose Burnside](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambrose_Burnside)'s [Fort Sanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Fort_Sanders).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell1981314-113] The capture of Chattanooga allowed Sherman to launch the [Atlanta campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlanta_campaign) from the city in May 1864.[^cite_note-114] The last major battles in the state came when Army of Tennessee regiments under [John Bell Hood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Bell_Hood) [invaded Middle Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin%E2%80%93Nashville_campaign) in the fall of 1864 in an effort to draw Sherman back. They were checked by [John Schofield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Schofield) at [Franklin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Franklin_\(1864\)) in November and completely dispersed by [George Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Henry_Thomas) at [Nashville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Nashville) in December.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell1981314–315-115] On April 27, 1865, the [worst maritime disaster in American history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_maritime_disasters) occurred when the *[Sultana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultana_\(steamboat\))* steamboat, which was transporting freed Union prisoners, [exploded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultana_\(steamboat\)#Disaster) in the Mississippi River north of Memphis, killing 1,168 people.[^cite_note-116]

When the [Emancipation Proclamation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emancipation_Proclamation) was announced, Tennessee was largely held by Union forces and thus not among the states enumerated, so it freed no slaves there.[^cite_note-npsjohnson-117] Andrew Johnson declared all slaves in Tennessee free on October 24, 1864.[^cite_note-npsjohnson-117] On February 22, 1865, the legislature approved an amendment to the state constitution prohibiting slavery, which was approved by voters the following month, and would go into effect later on in the year. This made Tennessee the only Southern state to abolish slavery at the time.[^cite_note-118][^cite_note-blackhistory-119] Tennessee ratified the [Thirteenth Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirteenth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution), which outlawed slavery in every state, on April 7, 1865.[^cite_note-120] On July 18, 1866, Tennessee became the first Confederate state to ratify the [Fourteenth Amendment](31666.md), which granted citizenship and equal protection under the law to former slaves. Both amendments went into effect after Tennessee's readmission to the Union because other states had not yet ratified them.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell1981333–334-121] Johnson became vice president when Lincoln was [reelected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1864_United_States_presidential_election), and president after Lincoln's [assassination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Abraham_Lincoln) in May 1865.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELangsdon2000131-105] On July 24, 1866, Tennessee became the first Confederate state to have its elected members readmitted to Congress.[^cite_note-122]

### Reconstruction and late 19th century

The years after the Civil War were characterized by tension and unrest between blacks and former Confederates, the worst of which occurred in [Memphis in 1866](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memphis_riots_of_1866).[^cite_note-123] Because Tennessee had ratified the Fourteenth Amendment before its readmission to the Union, it was the only former secessionist state that did not have a military governor during [Reconstruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconstruction_Era).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell1981333–334-121] However, federal troops would remain in Tennessee following the state's readmission and throughout Reconstruction in order to protect federal property, enforce laws, and keep the peace. The [Radical Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radical_Republicans) seized control of the state government toward the end of the war, and appointed [William G. "Parson" Brownlow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_G._Brownlow) governor. Under Brownlow's administration from 1865 to 1869, the legislature allowed African American men to vote, disenfranchised former Confederates, and with martial law, took action against the [Ku Klux Klan](16779.md), which was founded in December 1865 in [Pulaski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulaski,_Tennessee) as a vigilante group to advance former Confederates' interests.[^cite_note-124] In 1870, [Southern Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Democrats) regained control of the state legislature,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELamon198046–48-125] and over the next two decades, passed [Jim Crow laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Crow_laws) to enforce [racial segregation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_segregation).[^cite_note-126] A total of 251 [lynchings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States), predominately of Black people, took place in Tennessee.[^cite_note-127][^cite_note-128]

1879 illustration of Memphis, showing the city's cotton industry

Memphis became known as the "Cotton Capital of the World" in the years following the Civil War

A number of epidemics swept through Tennessee in the years after the Civil War, including [cholera](7591.md) in 1873, which devastated the Nashville area,[^cite_note-129] and [yellow fever](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_fever) in 1878, which killed more than one-tenth of Memphis's residents.[^cite_note-130][^cite_note-131] Reformers worked to modernize Tennessee into a "[New South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_South)" economy during this time. With the help of Northern investors, Chattanooga became one of the first industrialized cities in the South.[^cite_note-jsh-132] Memphis became known as the "Cotton Capital of the World" during the late 19th century, and Nashville, Knoxville, and several smaller cities saw modest industrialization.[^cite_note-jsh-132] Northerners also began exploiting the coalfields and mineral resources in the Appalachian Mountains. To pay off debts and alleviate overcrowded prisons, the state turned to [convict leasing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convict_leasing), providing prisoners to mining companies as [strikebreakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strikebreakers), which was protested by miners forced to compete with the system.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell1981387–389-133] An armed uprising in the Cumberland Mountains known as the [Coal Creek War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal_Creek_War) in 1891 and 1892 resulted in the state ending convict leasing.[^cite_note-134][^cite_note-135]

Despite New South promoters' efforts, agriculture continued to dominate Tennessee's economy.[^cite_note-136] The majority of freed slaves were forced into [sharecropping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharecropping) during the latter 19th century, and many others worked as agricultural wage laborers.[^cite_note-137] In 1897, Tennessee celebrated its statehood centennial one year late with the [Tennessee Centennial and International Exposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_Centennial_and_International_Exposition) in Nashville.[^cite_note-138] A [full-scale replica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parthenon_\(Nashville\)) of the [Parthenon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parthenon) in [Athens](1216.md) was designed by architect [William Crawford Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Crawford_Smith) and constructed for the celebration, owing to the city's reputation as the "Athens of the South".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell1981411–414-139][^cite_note-140]

### Early 20th century

Photograph of workers at Norris Dam in 1933

Workers at the [Norris Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norris_Dam) construction camp site in 1933

Due to increasing racial segregation and poor standards of living, many black Tennesseans fled to industrial cities in the Northeast and Midwest as part of the first wave of the [Great Migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Migration_\(African_American\)) between 1915 and 1930.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELamon198075–80-141] Many residents of rural parts of Tennessee relocated to larger cities during this time for more lucrative employment opportunities.[^cite_note-jsh-132] As part of the [Temperance movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperance_movement), Tennessee became the first state in the nation to effectively ban the sale, transportation, and production of alcohol in a series of laws passed between 1907 and 1917.[^cite_note-dickinson-142] During [Prohibition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prohibition_in_the_United_States), illicit production of [moonshine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moonshine) became extremely common in East Tennessee, particularly in the mountains, and continued for many decades afterward.[^cite_note-143]

Sgt. [Alvin C. York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alvin_York) of [Fentress County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fentress_County,_Tennessee) became one of the most famous and honored American soldiers of [World War I](4764461.md). He received the Congressional [Medal of Honor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medal_of_Honor) for single-handedly capturing an entire German machine gun regiment during the [Meuse–Argonne offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meuse%E2%80%93Argonne_offensive).[^cite_note-144] On July 9, 1918, Tennessee suffered the [worst rail accident in U.S. history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Train_Wreck_of_1918) when two passenger trains [collided head on](1803364.md) in Nashville, killing 101 and injuring 171.[^cite_note-Coggins2012-145] On August 18, 1920, Tennessee became the 36th and final state necessary to ratify the [Nineteenth Amendment to the United States Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nineteenth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution), which gave women the [right to vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_suffrage).[^cite_note-146] In 1925, [John T. Scopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_T._Scopes), a high school teacher in [Dayton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dayton,_Tennessee), was [tried and convicted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scopes_Trial) for teaching [evolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolution) in violation of the state's recently passed [Butler Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butler_Act).[^cite_note-147] Scopes was prosecuted by former [Secretary of State](32293.md) and presidential candidate [William Jennings Bryan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Jennings_Bryan) and defended by attorney [Clarence Darrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarence_Darrow). The case was intentionally publicized,[^cite_note-148] and highlighted the [creationism-evolution controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rejection_of_evolution_by_religious_groups) among religious groups.[^cite_note-149] In 1926, Congress authorized the establishment of [a national park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Smoky_Mountains_National_Park) in the [Great Smoky Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Smoky_Mountains), which was officially established in 1934 and dedicated in 1940.[^cite_note-150]

When the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression) struck in 1929, much of Tennessee was severely impoverished even by national standards.[^cite_note-151] As part of President [Franklin D. Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt)'s [New Deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Deal), the [Tennessee Valley Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_Valley_Authority) (TVA) was created in 1933 to provide electricity, jobs, flood control, improved waterway navigation, agricultural development, and economic modernization to the [Tennessee River Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_Valley).[^cite_note-clemnelson-152] The TVA built several hydroelectric dams in the state in the 1930s and 1940s, which inundated communities and thousands of farmland acreage, and forcibly displaced families via [eminent domain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eminent_domain_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-natarchTVA-153][^cite_note-stephens-154] The agency quickly grew into the country's largest electric utility and initiated a period of dramatic economic growth and transformation that brought many new industries and employment opportunities to the state.[^cite_note-clemnelson-152][^cite_note-155]

Photograph of calutron operators at Oak Ridge during the Manhattan Project

[Calutron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calutron) operators at the [Y-12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Y-12_National_Security_Complex) Plant in Oak Ridge during the [Manhattan Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manhattan_Project)

During [World War II](32927.md), East Tennessee was chosen for the production of [fissile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fissile) weapons-grade [enriched uranium](37555.md) as part of the [Manhattan Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manhattan_Project), a [research and development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_and_development) undertaking led by the U.S. to produce the world's first [atomic bombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_bomb). The [planned community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planned_community) of [Oak Ridge](151055.md) was built to provide accommodations for the facilities and workers; the site was chosen due to the abundance of TVA electric power, its low population density, and its inland geography and topography, which allowed for the natural separation of the facilities and a low vulnerability to attack.[^cite_note-156][^cite_note-157] The [Clinton Engineer Works](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinton_Engineer_Works) was established as the production arm of the Manhattan Project in Oak Ridge, which enriched uranium at three major facilities for use in atomic bombs. The first of the bombs was detonated in [Alamogordo, New Mexico](125944.md), in a test code-named [Trinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity_\(nuclear_test\)), and the second, nicknamed "[Little Boy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Boy)", was [dropped on Imperial Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_bombings_of_Hiroshima_and_Nagasaki) at the end of World War II.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJones1985522-158] After the war, the [Oak Ridge National Laboratory](38147.md) became an institution for scientific and technological research.[^cite_note-bigproblems-159]

### Mid-20th century to present

After the U.S. Supreme Court ruled racial segregation in public schools unconstitutional in *[Brown v. Board of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_v._Board_of_Education)* in 1954, [Oak Ridge High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oak_Ridge_High_School_\(Tennessee\)) in 1955 became the first school in Tennessee to be [integrated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_integration_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELamon1980100–101-160] The next year, nearby [Clinton High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinton_High_School_\(Clinton,_Tennessee\)) was integrated, and [Tennessee National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_National_Guard) troops were sent in after pro-segregationists threatened violence.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELamon1980100–101-160] Between February and May 1960, a [series of sit-ins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nashville_sit-ins) at segregated lunch counters in Nashville organized by the [Nashville Student Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nashville_Student_Movement) resulted in the desegregation of facilities in the city.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELamon1980106–108-161] On April 4, 1968, [James Earl Ray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Earl_Ray) [assassinated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Martin_Luther_King_Jr.) civil rights leader [Martin Luther King Jr.](20076.md) in Memphis.[^cite_note-162] King had traveled there to support [striking African American sanitation workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memphis_sanitation_strike).[^cite_note-163][^cite_note-164]

Photograph of the 1982 World's Fair in Knoxville, showing the Sunsphere

The [1982 World's Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1982_World's_Fair) in Knoxville

The 1962 [U.S. Supreme Court](31737.md) case *[Baker v. Carr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baker_v._Carr)* arose from a challenge to the longstanding rural bias of apportionment of seats in the Tennessee legislature and established the principle of "[one man, one vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_man,_one_vote)".[^cite_note-165][^cite_note-166] The construction of [Interstate 40](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_40_in_Tennessee) through Memphis became a national talking point on the issue of [eminent domain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eminent_domain_in_the_United_States) and [grassroots lobbying](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grassroots_lobbying) when the [Tennessee Department of Transportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_Department_of_Transportation) (TDOT) attempted to construct the highway through the city's [Overton Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overton_Park). A [local activist group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizens_to_Preserve_Overton_Park) spent many years contesting the project, and in 1971, the U.S. Supreme Court sided with the group and established the framework for [judicial review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_review_in_the_United_States) of government agencies in the [landmark case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_landmark_court_decisions_in_the_United_States) of *[Citizens to Preserve Overton Park v. Volpe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizens_to_Preserve_Overton_Park_v._Volpe)*.[^cite_note-interstate100-167][^cite_note-168] TVA's construction of the [Tellico Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tellico_Dam) in Loudon County became the subject of national controversy in the 1970s when the endangered [snail darter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snail_darter) fish was reported to be affected by the project. After lawsuits by environmental groups, the debate was decided by the U.S. Supreme Court case *[Tennessee Valley Authority v. Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_Valley_Authority_v._Hill)* in 1978, leading to amendments of the [Endangered Species Act](532055.md).[^cite_note-169]

Whitewater slalom contestants on the Ocoee River during the 1996 Olympics

The [Ocoee River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ocoee_River) was home to the [1996 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_Summer_Olympics) whitewater slalom events, the only Olympic sporting event ever held in the state.

The [1982 World's Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1982_World's_Fair) was held in Knoxville.[^cite_note-170] Also known as the Knoxville International Energy Exposition, the fair's theme was "Energy Turns the World". The exposition was one of the most successful, and the most recent world's fair to be held in the U.S.[^cite_note-171] In 1986, Tennessee held a yearlong celebration of the state's heritage and culture called "Homecoming '86".[^cite_note-172][^cite_note-173] Tennessee celebrated its bicentennial in 1996 with a yearlong celebration called "Tennessee 200". A new state park that traces the state's history, [Bicentennial Mall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicentennial_Mall_State_Park), was opened at the foot of Capitol Hill in Nashville.[^cite_note-174] The same year, the [whitewater slalom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canoe_slalom) events at the Atlanta [Summer Olympic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_Summer_Olympics) were held on the [Ocoee River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ocoee_River) in [Polk County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polk_County,_Tennessee).[^cite_note-fontenay-175]

In 2002, Tennessee amended its constitution to establish a [lottery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lotteries_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-176] In 2006, the state constitution [was amended](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_Marriage_Protection_Amendment) to outlaw [same-sex marriage](92656.md). This amendment was invalidated by the 2015 U.S. Supreme Court case *[Obergefell v. Hodges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obergefell_v._Hodges)*.[^cite_note-177] On December 23, 2008, the [largest industrial waste spill in United States history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingston_Fossil_Plant_coal_fly_ash_slurry_spill) occurred at TVA's [Kingston Fossil Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingston_Fossil_Plant) when more than 1.1 billion gallons of [coal ash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fly_ash) slurry was accidentally released into the [Emory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emory_River) and [Clinch Rivers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinch_River).[^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179] The cleanup cost more than \$1 billion and lasted until 2015.[^cite_note-180]

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Tennessee)

See also: [Geology of Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geology_of_Tennessee)







Maps of the [Grand Divisions of Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Divisions_of_Tennessee), with [East Tennessee](646800.md) at the top, [Middle Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Tennessee) in the center, and [West Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Tennessee) at the bottom.

Tennessee is in the [Southeastern United States](816925.md). Culturally, most of the state is considered part of the [Upland South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upland_South), and the eastern third is part of [Appalachia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appalachia).[^cite_note-181] Tennessee covers roughly 42,143 square miles (109,150 km<sup>2</sup>), of which 926 square miles (2,400 km<sup>2</sup>), or 2.2%, is water. It is the [16th smallest state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_area) in land area. The state is about 440 miles (710 km) long from east to west and 112 miles (180 km) wide from north to south. Tennessee is geographically, culturally, economically, and legally divided into three [Grand Divisions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Divisions_of_Tennessee): [East Tennessee](646800.md), [Middle Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Tennessee), and [West Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Tennessee).[^cite_note-granddivisions-182] It borders eight other states: [Kentucky](16846.md) and [Virginia](32432.md) to the north, [North Carolina](21650.md) to the east, [Georgia](48830.md), [Alabama](303.md), and [Mississippi](16949861.md) on the south, and [Arkansas](1930.md) and [Missouri](19571.md) on the west. It is tied with Missouri as the state bordering the most other states.[^cite_note-183] Tennessee is trisected by the [Tennessee River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_River), and its geographical center is in [Murfreesboro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murfreesboro,_Tennessee). Nearly three–fourths of the state is in the [Central Time Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Time_Zone), with most of East Tennessee on [Eastern Time](496028.md).[^cite_note-astor-184] The Tennessee River forms most of the division between Middle and West Tennessee.[^cite_note-granddivisions-182]

Tennessee's eastern boundary roughly follows the highest crests of the [Blue Ridge Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Ridge_Mountains), and the [Mississippi River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_River) forms its western boundary.[^cite_note-stein-185] Due to flooding of the Mississippi that has changed its path, the state's western boundary deviates from the river in some places.[^cite_note-tnmag0115-186] The northern border was originally defined as [36°30′ north latitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parallel_36%C2%B030%E2%80%B2_north) and the [Royal Colonial Boundary of 1665](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Colonial_Boundary_of_1665), but due to faulty surveys, begins north of this line in the east, and to the west, gradually veers north before shifting south onto the actual 36°30′ parallel at the Tennessee River in West Tennessee.[^cite_note-stein-185][^cite_note-187] Uncertainties in the latter 19th century over the location of the state's border with Virginia culminated in the U.S. Supreme Court [settling the matter in 1893](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_v._Tennessee), which resulted in the division of [Bristol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol,_Tennessee) between the two states.[^cite_note-188] An 1818 survey erroneously placed Tennessee's southern border 1 mile (1.6 km) south of the [35th parallel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/35th_parallel_north); Georgia legislators [continue to dispute this placement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee%E2%80%93Georgia_water_dispute), as it prevents Georgia from accessing the Tennessee River.[^cite_note-189]

Marked by a diversity of landforms and topographies, Tennessee features six principal [physiographic provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physiographic_regions_of_the_United_States), from east to west, which are part of three larger regions: the [Blue Ridge Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Ridge_Mountains), [Ridge-and-Valley Appalachians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ridge-and-Valley_Appalachians), and [Cumberland Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumberland_Plateau), part of the [Appalachian Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appalachian_Mountains); the [Highland Rim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highland_Rim) and [Nashville Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nashville_Basin), part of the [Interior Low Plateaus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interior_Low_Plateaus) of the [Interior Plains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interior_Plains); and the [East Gulf Coastal Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_Coastal_Plain), part of the [Atlantic Plains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_Plain).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESafford186911–12-190][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoore199455–56-191] Other regions include the southern tip of the [Cumberland Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumberland_Mountains), the [Western Tennessee Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_Valley), and the [Mississippi Alluvial Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_Alluvial_Plain). The state's highest point, which is also the third-highest peak in eastern North America, is [Kuwohi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuwohi) (formerly Clingmans Dome), at 6,643 feet (2,025 m) above sea level.[^cite_note-usgs-192] Its lowest point, 178 feet (54 m), is on the Mississippi River at the Mississippi state line in Memphis.[^cite_note-USGS-5] Tennessee has the most caves in the United States, with more than 10,000 documented.[^cite_note-193]

Geological formations in Tennessee largely correspond with the state's topographic features, and, in general, decrease in age from east to west. The state's oldest rocks are [igneous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igneous_rock) strata more than 1 billion years old found in the Blue Ridge Mountains,[^cite_note-194][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoore199426–28-195] and the youngest deposits in Tennessee are sands and silts in the Mississippi Alluvial Plain and river valleys that drain into the Mississippi River.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoore199449–52-196] Tennessee is considered seismically active and contains two major seismic zones, although destructive earthquakes rarely occur there.[^cite_note-197][^cite_note-198] The [Eastern Tennessee seismic zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Tennessee_seismic_zone) spans the entirety of East Tennessee from northwestern Alabama to southwestern Virginia, and is considered one of the most active zones in the Southeastern United States, frequently producing low-magnitude earthquakes.[^cite_note-199] The [New Madrid seismic zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Madrid_seismic_zone) in the northwestern part of the state produced [a series of devastating earthquakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1811%E2%80%931812_New_Madrid_earthquakes) between December 1811 and February 1812 that formed [Reelfoot Lake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reelfoot_Lake) near [Tiptonville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiptonville,_Tennessee).[^cite_note-200]

### Topography

Photograph of Mount Le Conte in the Great Smoky Mountains, the tallest mountain in eastern North America, measured from base to summit

[Mount Le Conte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Le_Conte_\(Tennessee\)) in the [Great Smoky Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Smoky_Mountains) is the tallest mountain in eastern North America, measured from base to summit

The southwestern [Blue Ridge Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Ridge_Mountains) lie within Tennessee's eastern edge, and are divided into several subranges, namely the [Great Smoky Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Smoky_Mountains), [Bald Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bald_Mountains), [Unicoi Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unicoi_Mountains), [Unaka Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unaka_Range), and [Iron Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Mountains). These mountains, which average 5,000 feet (1,500 m) above sea level in Tennessee, contain some of the highest elevations in eastern North America. The state's border with North Carolina roughly follows the highest peaks of this range, including Kuwohi. Most of the Blue Ridge area is protected by the [Cherokee National Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cherokee_National_Forest), the [Great Smoky Mountains National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Smoky_Mountains_National_Park), and several federal wilderness areas and state parks.[^cite_note-federal_lands-201] The [Appalachian Trail](87009.md) roughly follows the North Carolina state line before shifting westward into Tennessee.[^cite_note-202]

Stretching west from the Blue Ridge Mountains for about 55 miles (89 km) are the [Ridge-and-Valley Appalachians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ridge-and-Valley_Appalachians), also known as the Tennessee Valley[^cite_note-203] or Great Valley of East Tennessee. This area consists of linear parallel ridges separated by valleys that trend northeast to southwest, the general direction of the entire Appalachian range.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoore199464-204] Most of these ridges are low, but some of the higher ones are commonly called mountains.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoore199464-204] Numerous tributaries join to form the Tennessee River in the Ridge and Valley region.[^cite_note-physiomap-205]

Photograph of Fall Creek Falls, the tallest waterfall in the eastern United States

[Fall Creek Falls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_Creek_Falls), the tallest waterfall in the eastern United States, is located on the Cumberland Plateau

The [Cumberland Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumberland_Plateau) rises to the west of the Tennessee Valley, with an average elevation of 2,000 feet (610 m).[^cite_note-maertens-206] This landform is part of the larger [Appalachian Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appalachian_Plateau) and consists mostly of flat-topped [tablelands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Table_\(landform\)).[^cite_note-npsgeo-207] The plateau's eastern edge is relatively distinct, but the western [escarpment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Escarpment) is irregular, containing several long, crooked stream valleys separated by rocky cliffs with numerous [waterfalls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waterfall).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoore199468–72-208] The [Cumberland Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumberland_Mountains), with peaks above 3,500 feet (1,100 m), comprise the northeastern part of the Appalachian Plateau in Tennessee, and the southeastern part of the Cumberland Plateau is divided by the [Sequatchie Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sequatchie_Valley).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoore199468–72-208] The [Cumberland Trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justin_P._Wilson_Cumberland_Trail_State_Park) traverses the eastern escarpment of the Cumberland Plateau and Cumberland Mountains.[^cite_note-209]

Photograph of Reelfoot Lake in West Tennessee, formed by the 1811–1812 New Madrid earthquakes

[Reelfoot Lake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reelfoot_Lake) in West Tennessee was formed by the [1811–1812 New Madrid earthquakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1811%E2%80%931812_New_Madrid_earthquakes)

West of the Cumberland Plateau is the [Highland Rim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highland_Rim), an elevated plain that surrounds the [Nashville Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nashville_Basin), a [geological dome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dome_\(geology\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESafford186981–82-210] Both of these physiographic provinces are part of the [Interior Low Plateaus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interior_Low_Plateaus) of the larger [Interior Plains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interior_Plains). The Highland Rim is Tennessee's largest geographic region, and is often split into eastern and western halves.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESafford186981–82,_103-211] The Eastern Highland Rim is characterized by relatively level plains dotted by rolling hills, and the Western Highland Rim and western Nashville Basin are covered with uneven rounded knobs with steep [ravines](2014227.md) separated by meandering streams.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESafford186983–85,_98–100-212] The Nashville Basin has rich, fertile farmland,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESafford186997-213] and porous limestone bedrock very close to the surface underlies both the Nashville Basin and Eastern Highland Rim.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESafford186983–85-214] This results in [karst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karst) that forms numerous caves, sinkholes, depressions, and underground streams.[^cite_note-mooredrumm-215]

West of the Highland Rim is the [Western Tennessee Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_Valley), which consists of about 10 miles (16 km) in width of hilly land along the banks of the Tennessee River.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESafford1869104–106-216] West of this is the [Gulf Coastal Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_Coastal_Plain), a broad feature that begins at the [Gulf of Mexico](21076367.md) and extends northward into southern [Illinois](14849.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESafford1869110–111-217] The plain begins in the east with low rolling hills and wide stream valleys, known as the West Tennessee Highlands, and gradually levels out to the west.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESafford1869111-218] It ends at steep [loess](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loess) bluffs overlooking the [Mississippi embayment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_embayment), the westernmost physiographic division of Tennessee, which is part of the larger [Mississippi Alluvial Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_Alluvial_Plain).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoore199482–84-219] This flat 10 to 14 miles (16 to 23 km) wide strip is commonly known as the Mississippi Bottoms, and contains lowlands, [floodplains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Floodplain), and [swamps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swamp).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESafford1869112–113-220][^cite_note-221]

### Hydrology



The [Tennessee River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_River) in the [Tennessee River Gorge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_River_Gorge)

Tennessee is drained by [three major rivers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_rivers_in_Tennessee), the [Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_River), [Cumberland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumberland_River), and [Mississippi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_River), and has the fifth-largest river system in the United States.[^cite_note-222] The Tennessee River begins at the juncture of the [Holston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holston_River) and [French Broad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Broad_River) rivers in Knoxville, flows southwest to Chattanooga, and exits into Alabama before reemerging in the western part of the state and flowing north into Kentucky.[^cite_note-loc39-223] Its major tributaries include the [Clinch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinch_River), [Little Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Tennessee_River), [Hiwassee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiwassee_River), [Sequatchie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sequatchie_River), [Elk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elk_River_\(Tennessee_River_tributary\)), [Beech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beech_River), [Buffalo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buffalo_River_\(Tennessee\)), [Duck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duck_River_\(Tennessee\)), and [Big Sandy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Sandy_River_\(Tennessee\)) rivers.[^cite_note-loc39-223] The Cumberland River flows through the north-central part of the state, emerging in the northeastern Highland Rim, passing through Nashville, turning northwest to Clarksville, and entering Kentucky east of the Tennessee River.[^cite_note-tdeccumberland-224] Its principal branches in Tennessee are the [Obey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obey_River), [Caney Fork](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caney_Fork_River), [Stones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stones_River), [Harpeth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harpeth_River), and [Red](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_River_\(Cumberland_River_tributary\)) rivers.[^cite_note-tdeccumberland-224] The Mississippi River drains nearly all of West Tennessee.[^cite_note-tdecms-225] Its tributaries are the [Obion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obion_River), [Forked Deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forked_Deer_River), [Hatchie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hatchie_River), [Loosahatchie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loosahatchie_River), and [Wolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolf_River_\(Tennessee\)) rivers.[^cite_note-tdecms-225] The [Tennessee Valley Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_Valley_Authority) (TVA) and the [U.S. Army Corps of Engineers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Army_Corps_of_Engineers) operate many hydroelectric dams on the Tennessee and Cumberland rivers and their tributaries, which form large reservoirs throughout the state.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorlewFolmsbeeMitchell198112–13-226]

About half the state's land area is in the [Tennessee Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_Valley) drainage basin of the Tennessee River.[^cite_note-loc39-223] The Cumberland River basin covers the northern half of Middle Tennessee and a small portion of East Tennessee.[^cite_note-tdeccumberland-224] A small part of north-central Tennessee is in the [Green River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_River_\(Kentucky\)) watershed.[^cite_note-227] All three of these basins are tributaries of the [Ohio River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohio_River) watershed. Most of West Tennessee is in the [Lower Mississippi River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Mississippi_River) watershed.[^cite_note-tdecms-225] The entirety of the state is in the [Mississippi River watershed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_River#Watershed), except for a small sliver near the southeastern corner traversed by the [Conasauga River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conasauga_River), which is part of the [Mobile Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mobile_Bay) watershed.[^cite_note-228]

### Ecology

Photograph of a cedar glade, a rare ecosystem found in Middle Tennessee

[Cedar glades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcareous_glade) are ecosystems found in regions of Middle Tennessee where limestone bedrock is close to the surface

Tennessee is within a [temperate deciduous forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate_deciduous_forest) biome commonly known as the Eastern Deciduous Forest.[^cite_note-229] It has eight [ecoregions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ecoregions_in_the_United_States_\(EPA\)): the [Blue Ridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Ridge_Mountains), [Ridge and Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ridge-and-Valley_Appalachians), Central Appalachian, Southwestern Appalachian, [Interior Low Plateaus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interior_Low_Plateaus), Southeastern Plains, Mississippi Valley Loess Plains, and [Mississippi Alluvial Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_Alluvial_Plain_\(ecoregion\)) regions.[^cite_note-fap20-230] Tennessee is the most [biodiverse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiverse) inland state,[^cite_note-231] the Great Smoky Mountains National Park is the most biodiverse national park,[^cite_note-climatechange-232][^cite_note-233] and the [Duck River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duck_River_\(Tennessee\)) is the most biologically diverse waterway in North America.[^cite_note-234] The Nashville Basin is renowned for its diversity of flora and fauna.[^cite_note-235] Tennessee is home to 340 species of birds, 325 [freshwater fish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freshwater_fish) species, 89 mammals, 77 amphibians, and 61 reptiles.[^cite_note-climatechange-232]

Forests cover about 52% of Tennessee's land area, with [oak–hickory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oak%E2%80%93hickory_forest) the dominant type.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESlaytonJohnsonGreeneHill20203-236] [Appalachian oak–pine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appalachian%E2%80%93Blue_Ridge_forests#Dry_oak%E2%80%93pine_forests) and [cove hardwood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cove_\(Appalachian_Mountains\)#Cove_forest) forests are found in the Blue Ridge Mountains and Cumberland Plateau, and [bottomland hardwood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bottomland_hardwood_forest) forests are common throughout the Gulf Coastal Plain.[^cite_note-ut95-237] [Pine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pine) forests are also found throughout the state.[^cite_note-ut95-237] The [Southern Appalachian spruce–fir forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Appalachian_spruce%E2%80%93fir_forest) in the highest elevations of the Blue Ridge Mountains is considered the second-most endangered ecosystem in the country.[^cite_note-238] Some of the last remaining large [American chestnut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_chestnut) trees grow in the Nashville Basin and are being used to help breed [blight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blight)-resistant trees.[^cite_note-239] Middle Tennessee is home to many unusual and rare ecosystems known as [cedar glades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcareous_glade), which occur in areas with shallow limestone bedrock that is largely barren of overlying soil and contain many endemic plant species.[^cite_note-240]

Common mammals found throughout Tennessee include [white-tailed deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White-tailed_deer), [red](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_fox) and [gray foxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gray_fox), [coyotes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coyote), [raccoons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raccoon), [opossums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opossum), [wild turkeys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wild_turkey), [rabbits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabbit), and [squirrels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Squirrel). [Black bears](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_black_bear) are found in the Blue Ridge Mountains and on the Cumberland Plateau. Tennessee has the third-highest number of amphibian species, with the Great Smoky Mountains home to the most [salamander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salamander) species in the world.[^cite_note-241] The state ranks second in the nation for the diversity of its freshwater fish species.[^cite_note-natureserve-242]

### Climate

See also: [Climate change in Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Tennessee)

refer to caption

[Köppen climate types](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification) of Tennessee, using 1991–2020 [climate normals](42778673.md).
